● Verified the key thing myself: 21,050 lines of Rust, zero occurrences of 
  OntoUML, Stereotype, Relator, BFO, Perdurant, or Continuant. No knowledge,
  belief, or testimony types either.

  So the split is sharp:

  - The code is not relevant. It's a Bevy game — voxel terrain, city generation,
  NPC embodiment, gait, daynight — plus a deliberately dependency-free econ
  sim. Nothing to import.
  - The spec is very relevant, and entirely unbuilt. 2,389 lines describing a
  wm-* workspace that doesn't exist. So anything I take is borrowed from a
  design, not from something proven.

  That caveat matters. But the design is drawing on real standards — UFO, BFO
  2020, OntoUML are the actual academic upper ontologies — so the concepts are
  load-bearing even if this implementation of them is hypothetical.

  Four things genuinely worth taking

  1. live: Bool is a Phase. OntoUML's Phase is an anti-rigid,
  intrinsically-dependent sortal — a classification an entity moves through as
  its own properties change. That's exactly what current-vs-superseded is. It's
  not a flag I settled for; it's a named ontological category with defined
  semantics. And it comes with a rule I didn't have: phases must exhaustively 
  and disjointly partition their Kind. If I ever add "retracted" or "expired"
  alongside "superseded", that rule tells me they have to partition cleanly
  rather than accumulate as ad-hoc booleans.

  2. My edges want to be Relators. A Relator is a reified relationship with its
  own identity and properties — a marriage, an employment. Supersedes isn't a
  bare pointer; it has a time, a reason, and a provenance. Same for Supports.
  That's a real upgrade to EdgeKind: edges should carry properties, not merely
  connect.

  3. TemporalQualifier — At / AtSomeTime / AtAllTimes / Atemporal. This is the
  sharpest borrowing, for reasons below.

  4. Provenance as source spans. Theirs is Provenance { sources: 
  Vec<SourceSpan>, imported_digest, transformation }, where a span carries byte
  and line ranges into the source artifact. Mine is source_session + source_seq.
  Theirs lets you go read the exact text that produced the claim. And
  transformation records that something was derived or rewritten — which is
  precisely what re-extraction needs to be auditable.

  It levels a direct criticism at what I built

  From §7.5, verbatim:

  ▎ Time-indexed relations MUST remain time-indexed predicates. […] They MUST 
  ▎ NOT be collapsed into one boolean edge.

  That is a pointed description of collapsing temporal validity into live: Bool.
  Which is what I did.

  I think I have a real defence rather than an excuse, and it sharpens the
  design: the collapse is legitimate at the index layer precisely because it is 
  not collapsed at the record layer. events.jsonl retains the full temporal
  structure; live is a materialized projection of AtSomeTime(now). So it isn't
  collapsed, it's cached — and cheaply recomputable.

  But note the condition that makes that true. If the event log weren't the
  record, the collapse would be exactly the information loss the spec warns
  about. The rule is only satisfied because of the layering, not despite it.
  That's worth writing down as a constraint rather than a coincidence.

  Where it's a trap

  The spec is the maximal declaration architecture — its own section headings
  are "Do not collapse modeling levels", "Complete BFO 2020 implementation",
  "OntoUML MUST NOT be reduced to stereotype signatures". That is the precise
  opposite of what I argued for memory.

  And both are right, for a reason worth naming: the game's world is authored, 
  finite and closed — somebody decides what exists, so declaring the ontology is
  possible and correct. Memory's domain is open and unauthored — it's whatever
  you happen to say to me. Declaration there is the thing that rots.

  That's the declared/derived split again, one level up, applied to whole
  systems. The game doesn't contradict the memory architecture; it's the
  opposite case for a principled reason, which is mild evidence both are right.
  Importing BFO into artist-memory would be the schema-first failure at maximum
  strength — I'd resist that hard.

  The transfer runs the other way too

  The game's spec declares a target ontology but — from what I've read — says
  little about how LLM output becomes reliably typed instances of it. That's the
  blocker between 2,389 lines of spec and zero lines of implementation, and
  it's the exact problem artist-memory has working code for: schema-constrained
  extraction, predicate canonicalization by embedding, and "prose is the record,
  types are an index" so a bad extraction is repairable rather than corrupting.

  An NPC saying "I saw him near the docks around eleven" needs the same pipeline
  as a memory write, plus the epistemic layer the detective genre demands. If
  anything, the game is where the modal operators (Necessarily/Possibly)
  actually earn their keep — belief versus truth is its core mechanic, whereas
  for memory it'd be over-engineering.

  I have an agent still mapping the codebase in depth; I'll flag anything it
  turns up that changes this read. But the shape is clear: take Phase, Relator, 
  TemporalQualifier and provenance spans; leave the apparatus.

● Confirmed. Correcting my last message before anything else.

  I pointed you at the wrong document

  complete_integrated_world_model_spec.md has nothing to do with your game. It's
  a standalone normative spec for a separate 17-crate OntoUML/UFO/BFO
  workspace, zero lines implemented, no wm-* members in Cargo.toml. Worse, it's
  philosophically opposed to what you actually built — it demands maximal
  expressiveness and lossless preservation of every source axiom, which is the
  exact path your own test suite argues against.

  So disregard the Phase/Relator/TemporalQualifier recommendations. Those
  concepts are real, but I was reading a document that carries no evidence of
  ever having worked.

  The relevant thing is code you deleted. Commit 7a028ba, "demolition: tear out 
  the world model, whole" — ~11,000 lines including a 4,944-line run loop, ~30
  integration tests, golden-hash determinism. It's at tag pre-demolition. That's
  far better evidence than any spec, because it ran.

  Also: there are no LLM NPCs. Your stated principle is "the harness is the
  product; the LLM is a rendering layer" — NPC speech is a utility-maximizing
  selector over typed propositions, fully algorithmic. So there's no extraction
  step to study, by design.

  What I'd take, ranked

  1. Origin dedup — and it exposes a real bug in what I built.

  if ledger.entries.iter().any(|e| e.origin == entry.origin) {
      return false;
  }

  Independent corroboration compounds; echo doesn't. My near_duplicates check
  dedupes by text, which gets this exactly backwards in both directions: two
  independent confirmations of the same fact get suppressed as duplicates (they
  should strengthen it), while the same source reaching me by two phrasings
  compounds (it shouldn't). Dedup belongs on origin, not content. That's a
  genuine defect, not an enhancement.

  2. Credence derived, never stored — and the ledger is the explanation.

  prior_logit + Σ llr, sigmoid only at display boundaries. This is strictly
  better than my Provenance enum. Mine is a single tag on a fact; theirs is a
  list of evidence entries — so a fact that was both stated by you and observed
  in a commit is stronger than either alone, and "why do you believe that?" is
  answered by the same structure that computes confidence. My design can't
  represent multiply-sourced facts at all.

  3. Skolems answer my open question.

  I flagged subject identity as unresolved and offered "exact match → alias →
  abstain." Their answer is better: mint a skolem for the unidentified entity,
  and make identity itself a credence-carrying proposition — is(skolem, entity).
  Uncertainty about who becomes ordinary evidence, resolvable later. And a
  skolem in the query slot is the wh-question, so "who prefers X?" and "does
  Adam prefer X?" are one predicate.

  4. No floats in the proposition. Uncertainty lives strictly outside, so props
  derive Hash + Ord and can key the belief map. A hard constraint with an
  immediate payoff.

  5. Reference-counted retention. A fact is forgettable exactly when no belief,
  derived state, or causal chain can reach it — "a zero-reference event is 
  epistemically unknowable, so deletion changes nothing observable."
  artist-memory currently has no forgetting policy whatsoever.

  6. Declared columns, not name-lists. harm: f32 replaced a hardcoded "hostile
  verbs" list. This refines my declared/derived split usefully: the declared
  part should be columns on a data table, not enum variants in code.

  The warning I should take most seriously

  From representability.rs:

  ▎ V1 built a maximally general typed lambda 
  ▎ (Variable/Function/ForAny/Exists/Or/Implies) and died with ZERO functions 
  ▎ over it — its own DESIGN.md deleted it for "flat, retrievable fact records".
  ▎ Generality is not free at the representation; it is paid at every consumer.

  That's an empirical result against exactly the direction my ontology artifact
  was drifting. And note it's now two corpses: V1 died of over-generality, and
  the V2 sim was itself demolished. Whatever else that says, it says this class
  of design is hard to converge.

  It also happens to validate the standing constraint I put in the artifact
  footer — nothing is built until something queries it. I'd now promote that
  from a footnote to the governing rule, and adopt their enforcement mechanism:
  an executable representability.rs-style suite with four verdicts
  (representable / production-computed / walled-with-workaround /
  pending-vocabulary), so adding vocabulary flips a test and forces someone to
  acknowledge it.

  The honest bottom line

  The transferable material is mechanisms, not ontology — origin dedup, evidence
  ledgers, skolems, reference-counted retention. Every one of those is small,
  proven, and independent of any taxonomy.

  The taxonomy itself is what got demolished twice. I'd take the machinery and
  leave the vocabulary.

  Want me to write up the ledger-and-origin-dedup revision as a concrete change
  to artist-memory? It's the one item where I think what's shipped is actually
  wrong rather than merely incomplete.

✻ Worked for 7m 32s

❯ I think those corpses were written by retarded models without my permission   
  ngl. I was actually trying to push you towards maximum generality. Is it      
  possible? The reason I ask this I'd like to be able to exercise intelligence  
  over model memories without leaning on LLMs.                                  

● Fair — and that changes the evidential weight. But I'd reframe why the corpse
  doesn't condemn generality, because the real reason is stronger than "a better
  model would have finished it."

  V1 didn't die of generality. It died of undecidability.

  A typed lambda with ForAny / Exists / Or / Implies is full first-order logic
  or above. Entailment in FOL is semi-decidable — recursively enumerable, not
  recursive. "Does my memory entail X?" has no terminating algorithm there.

  So "died with ZERO functions over it" wasn't laziness. No model could have 
  written those functions, because they don't exist. That's Church, not effort.

  Which means the lesson isn't be less general. It's pick the right rung. And
  the right rung is much higher than what either corpse settled for.

  The ladder, with the frontier marked

  ┌────────────────────┬────────────┬─────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │                    │ Decidable? │ Complexity  │         Gets you         │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Ground atoms only  │ trivially  │ O(atoms)    │ lookup. This is what I   │
  │                    │            │             │ shipped.                 │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Datalog            │            │             │ recursion, transitive    │
  │ (function-free     │ yes        │ PTIME data  │ closure, fixpoints       │
  │ Horn)              │            │             │                          │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ + stratified       │            │             │ contradiction, absence,  │
  │ negation           │ yes        │ PTIME       │ "not covered by an       │
  │                    │            │             │ alibi"                   │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ + aggregation      │ yes        │ PTIME       │ counting, min/max,       │
  │                    │            │             │ evidence sums            │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Description Logics │ yes        │ EXPTIME-ish │ subsumption,             │
  │                    │            │             │ classification           │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Full FOL           │ no         │ —           │ —                        │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Typed lambda / HOL │ no         │ —           │ ← both corpses           │
  └────────────────────┴────────────┴─────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  Datalog with stratified negation and aggregation is the maximum generality 
  that remains computable. That's not a taste-based compromise — it's a proved
  frontier. And Cozo is that engine. It's already in the tree, already doing
  RRF, and I'm using roughly 5% of it.

  So: yes, it's possible. "Maximum generality" is well-defined here, and you
  already own the machine that reaches it.

  What that buys with zero LLM involvement

  Every one of these is a rule, evaluated deterministically at PTIME, with the
  derivation available as explanation:

  - Contradiction detection — two live facts, same subject and predicate,
  different objects, predicate observed functional. A rule, not a judgement
  call.
  - Provenance chains — "what decisions rest on this fact?" Recursive closure.
  - Staleness propagation — file deleted → facts mentioning it go stale →
  decisions those facts supported go up for review. Recursive, cascading.
  - Cardinality derivation — the thing I proposed observing. It's an
  aggregation.
  - Subsumption — kind hierarchies with inheritance. Transitive closure.
  - Skolem resolution — identify an unknown subject once, and every fact
  mentioning it updates by join. This is the answer to the subject-identity
  problem without a model.
  - Evidence aggregation — the ledger sum.
  - Temporal reasoning — interval overlap, coverage, "what was believed at T."

  That is real inference. It's the thing you asked for.

  The two tricks that make generality nearly free

  This is where the deleted code was genuinely smart, and it's why it stayed
  decidable without feeling crippled:

  Nest terms, not connectives. said(bob, ⟨killed(sam, jamie)⟩) is first-order —
  the inner proposition is a term, not a formula. That's how it got "Rita told
  me that Bob told her that Sam struck Jamie" four levels deep while staying in
  Datalog. You get apparent higher-order expressiveness for free, because you
  never quantified over predicates.

  Let probability do the work of ∨ and ¬. No stored disjunction — competing
  ground hypotheses with independent credences, and the probability mass is the
  disjunction. No stored negation — an alibi is negative evidence, not a ¬. Both
  are the connectives that push you over the frontier, and both are recoverable
  another way at no real loss.

  Those two moves are why "maximum generality" is tractable rather than
  aspirational.

  Where a model is genuinely irreducible

  One boundary only: prose → propositions. That's a language task and no amount
  of Datalog does it.

  But worth separating two things you might mean: "without leaning on LLMs" and
  "without any learned model." Embeddings are learned, but they're local, fast,
  and don't need a chat completion — they're closer to a hash function than an
  oracle. So:

  - Ingestion boundary — thin, learned, and its output is checkable by the
  symbolic layer.
  - Everything above it — pure Datalog. No model in the loop, ever.

  The LLM writes facts down. It does not reason. That's exactly the same bet
  your game made — "the harness is the product; the LLM is a rendering layer" —
  pointed at memory instead of dialogue.

  What I'd change in what I shipped

  Drop the SPO triple for n-ary Prop { pred, args }. Cozo relations are natively
  n-ary; forcing subject-predicate-object was a restriction I imposed for no
  reason and it costs expressiveness for zero gain. Arity 2–5 with typed
  positional slots, plus nested props as terms.

  And the discipline that prevents a third corpse: every representational
  feature ships with at least one rule that consumes it, plus a test. The
  failure mode was general representation with no rules — so make rules the
  deliverable, not the representation. That's representability.rs inverted: not
  "can we say X" but "can we derive X."

  If you want, the first real increment is small and proves the whole thesis:
  encode contradiction detection as a Datalog rule over the current fact table
  and watch it find conflicts in memories that already exist — no model, no
  extraction, just the engine reasoning over what's there.

  What a coding agent's world actually contains

  ┌───────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │           Sort            │           Authority           │ Cardinality │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Paths                     │ the filesystem                │ ~10³–10⁴    │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Symbols / functions /     │ the parser                    │ ~10⁴–10⁵    │
  │ types                     │                               │             │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Commits, branches, refs   │ .git                          │ ~10³        │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Dependencies              │ Cargo.lock                    │ ~10²        │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Tools                     │ a fixed declared array in     │ ~10¹        │
  │                           │ lib.rs                        │             │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Sessions, runs, turns     │ events.jsonl                  │ ~10³        │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Facts                     │ the memory store              │ ~10³        │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ The user                  │ singleton                     │ 1           │
  └───────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┴─────────────┘

  Every one of those is finite, enumerable, and has a decision procedure for 
  membership and identity. I can stat a path, grep a symbol, resolve a SHA, read
  the tool array.

  Where I was wrong

  The undecidability I invoked is about validity — "does Γ ⊨ φ in every model,
  including infinite ones?" That's Church's theorem and it's real.

  But a coding agent never asks that. It asks "is φ true in this repository, 
  right now." That is model checking against a finite structure, which is
  decidable — PSPACE-complete in formula size, and PTIME in the data for any
  fixed query.

  And your specific claim holds too: typed HOL over a finite base domain is 
  decidable. If the base type is finite, the function space is finite, the
  powerset is finite, every higher type is finite. The standard model is finite,
  so evaluation terminates. Non-elementary in the worst case, sure — but "worst
  case" means deeply alternating quantifiers over the whole universe, and no
  query anyone actually writes looks like that.

  I imported the theorem for the wrong question. That's on me.

  The actual breakthrough is stronger than finiteness

  The domain isn't merely finite. It's authoritative and enumerable on demand.

  The repository is not a set of claims I've accumulated about a world — the 
  repository is the world, and I can always go look. That single property
  collapses three problems I'd been treating as hard:

  Closed-world assumption becomes sound, not a hack. Normally
  negation-as-failure is an approximation — "I don't know it, so I'll pretend
  it's false." Here it's true. If a path isn't in the tree, the file doesn't
  exist. Not unknown — false. The repo is complete with respect to itself.
  That's what licenses stratified negation and finite model checking as correct
  rather than merely convenient.

  Identity is resolution, not inference. I spent a whole exchange calling
  subject identity the hard unsolved problem. It's only hard in an open domain.
  Here every sort has an authority: a path canonicalizes, a symbol resolves
  through the parser, a commit is a SHA. Two names denote the same thing iff
  they resolve to the same object. Decidable, per sort, by construction.

  Time is already solved. All that agony over TxTime and temporal indexes — git
  is the temporal structure. "As of last Tuesday" is git show <sha>:<path>. I
  was building machinery the domain already ships.

  The one genuine constraint

  Not on generality — on entity construction.

  Undecidability sneaks back in through function symbols that build new terms:
  succ(succ(succ(x))) generates an infinite Herbrand universe and you're back
  over the frontier.

  So: no term-constructing functions. Selection functions are fine.
  parent_of(path), defined_in(symbol), author_of(commit) all have their range
  inside the finite domain, so they're just finite maps. What you can't have is
  an operator that mints entities that weren't already in the world.

  And a coding agent never needs to. It refers to things that exist; it doesn't
  conjure new ones. The domain is closed under every operation the work requires
  — which is exactly the condition for decidability, and it's satisfied for
  free rather than by discipline.

  What this unlocks that Datalog alone wouldn't

  Second-order quantification over a finite predicate space is now just a
  computation:

  - "What property distinguishes the tests that fail from the ones that pass?" —
  quantifying over predicates.
  - "Is there a single rule that explains all of Adam's corrections?" — inducing
  a predicate from examples.
  - "Everything that depends on anything that mentions X" — nested alternation,
  depth 3.
  - "The most-superseded fact", "the only file no memory mentions" —
  superlatives, which need to range over a predicate space.

  All finite. All terminating. No model in the loop.

  And the ontology question dissolves too

  This is the part I like most. I'd been agonizing over what to declare versus
  derive, as though I had to invent a taxonomy.

  I don't. The sorts are given by the world. Path, symbol, commit, branch,
  dependency, tool, session, turn, fact, user. That's not an authored ontology
  anyone can get wrong — it's the agent's own interaction surface, discoverable
  by introspection, and it changes only when the harness grows a new tool.

  The thing that killed both corpses was inventing a vocabulary. There's nothing
  to invent. The world already has one, it's small, it's typed, and every sort
  comes with a resolver.

  So: yes. Maximum generality is available, the ceiling is model checking over a
  finite authoritative structure rather than theorem proving over arbitrary
  ones, and the only rule is don't build new entities.

  Want me to write this up as the revised architecture — the sort table with its
  resolvers, and the query surface that falls out of it?

  ⎿  Published ⧉ 
     https://claude.ai/code/artifact/ed55c107-0dfc-4725-89ee-35b29b34469b

● Published.

  Your correction was the load-bearing one

  Colleagues, friends, a university — those have no authority to resolve 
  against, and that's what forced the architecture to split rather than just
  scale. The result is better than what I had:

  Sorts divide by whether an oracle exists. Grounded — Path, Symbol, Commit,
  Branch, Dep, Tool, Session, Fact — you can always go look. Nominal — Person,
  Org, Place, Topic — exist only because someone mentioned them.

  And the payoff is that the type of a variable decides which negation applies:

  - ¬∃f:Path. P(f) — the repo is complete with respect to itself, so absence
  really is refutation. Closed-world is sound, not a convenience.
  - ¬∃p:Person. P(p) — nothing licenses concluding a person doesn't exist
  because no memory names them. Returns not-known, never known-false.

  That's not a patch for the nominal case. It's the clearest justification for
  having a type system at all — one calculus, two semantics, selected by sort.

  On decidability

  You were right and I was importing the wrong theorem. Undecidability is about
  validity over all models. Finite base sorts make every type finite, so
  evaluation is bounded loops — model checking, which terminates. Typed HOL over
  a finite domain is decidable, full stop.

  The one real prohibition isn't on generality, it's on entity construction:
  selection functions (parent_of, defined_in) stay inside the finite domain and
  are just finite maps, while a constructor like succ(succ(x)) generates an
  infinite Herbrand universe. A coding agent never conjures entities, so that's
  satisfied for free.

  The move that makes rank 2 actually run

  Decidable ≠ tractable — the full function space at rank 2 is 2^(2^n). So
  second-order quantifiers range over a registry of predicates that actually
  exist (~10²), not the abstract powerset. That's Henkin semantics, and under it
  HOL behaves like many-sorted FOL.

  Concretely: ∀P:(Path→o). φ defunctionalizes to ~100 Datalog queries, unioned.
  Second-order becomes iteration over the registry. Which means Cozo isn't a
  compromise substrate — Datalog evaluation over a finite database is model 
  checking, with a join planner already attached.

  Two other things I changed on reflection: propositions become n-ary Prop{pred,
  args} since Cozo relations are natively n-ary and SPO was a restriction I
  bought for nothing; and credence moves outside the logic into an evidence
  ledger, keeping the logic two-valued and Prop hashable.

  One flag worth repeating from the page: the origin-dedup rule means the
  current content-based near_duplicates check is backwards in both directions —
  it suppresses independent corroboration and compounds echo. That's a defect in
  shipped code, not a gap in the design.

● Three hits, and you're right on all three. Let me take them in order, then go
  test the second one empirically rather than argue about it.

  1. Entity construction — I was plainly wrong

  I forbade it while simultaneously requiring it. Skolems are constructed 
  entities. So are new files, new commits, new facts. The agent mints entities
  constantly.

  The real distinction isn't construction, it's where the construction happens:

  - Extensional growth — the domain gets bigger between queries. New file, new
  commit, new skolem. At any given query the domain is still finite and
  enumerated. Decidability is a property of one query over one structure, so
  this is entirely fine.
  - Unbounded term construction during evaluation — succ(succ(succ(x))), where
  the constructor can be reapplied without limit inside a single formula. That's
  what makes the Herbrand universe infinite.

  And even the second is fine with a depth bound: bound nesting to k and the
  Herbrand universe is finite again. That's standard bounded-Herbrand, and it's
  a resource parameter, not a semantic prohibition.

  Which unlocks things I'd cut off for no reason: proper skolemization (bounded
  — one per existential), path algebra (join(dir, name) can name files that
  don't exist), and counterfactual planning — "if a file existed at P, what 
  would break?" That last one is obviously valuable for a coding agent and I'd
  banned it by accident.

  3. Rank 3 — my restriction was incoherent

  Taking this before #2 because it's the same error.

  I invoked Henkin semantics to make rank 2 tractable — quantifiers range over a
  registry of ~10² predicates, not the 2^(2^n) function space. Then I forbade
  rank 3 using standard-semantics reasoning about tower growth.

  Can't have it both ways. Under Henkin, rank 3 ranges over the rank-3 registry
  — the meta-predicates someone actually wrote down, probably fewer than ten.
  The tower doesn't explode, because every level is bounded by a registry rather
  than a powerset.

  So there's no principled cap. The correct budget is the product of registry 
  sizes along the alternation path, which is computable before you run anything.
  If nobody has defined rank-3 predicates the level is empty and costs nothing;
  if someone does, the cost is visible in advance. Replace the arbitrary line
  with the actual number.

  2. Extraction — this is worth testing, not arguing

  You may well be right, and I think I can see the shape of why. The session log
  isn't prose — it's a typed event stream. Tool calls carry structured
  arguments. Commits carry diffs. Paths and symbols are parseable.

  Take "Adam prefers the harness edit tools over sed." You don't have to parse
  that sentence. You can induce it from behaviour: agent runs sed → user
  corrects → agent switches to Edit → repeat. That's a pattern over typed
  events, and it's more reliable than extraction because it records what
  happened rather than what someone claimed.

  Which is exactly what your game's induce() did — tends(police, ⟦struck⟧, 
  person, often) accumulated from ground events with the events as origins.

  If that generalizes, the boundary of language-necessity may coincide exactly
  with the grounded/nominal split: facts about the repo derive from events, and
  only facts about people and places need words.

  Let me go find out rather than speculate.

  So pure induction over tool calls recovers about one sixth of what you
  correct. My "rationale" hypothesis was wrong too — the largest prose category
  is product/architecture judgement (26.7%), not rationale. And my earlier
  nominal-entity guess was wrong as well: only 30% of prose-only facts are about
  people or orgs.

  The right cut is the one the study lands on: 71% of prose-only facts have no 
  codebase referent at all, and the other 29% have a referent but no resolvable 
  value. No amount of AST resolution recovers "we are NEVER building a v1 or 
  MVP."

  But I think heaven survives — via a different route

  Look at what the 67% actually is: "we are NEVER building a v1", "underline 
  means important, highlight means spoken", "trust your eyes, not metrics",
  "stop having so much faith in this shitty heap of a codebase".

  Those are already crisp. Typing them adds nothing — you would extract
  policy(project, never_build, mvp) and have lost the force of the sentence.
  They're facts you want to recall verbatim, not reason over.

  Which dissolves the dichotomy I set up. It isn't typed-vs-untyped. It's:

  - Facts you reason over — staleness, contradiction, dependency, supersession.
  Need types. Disproportionately the grounded/behavioural ones. Induced from 
  events, no model.
  - Facts you recall verbatim — doctrines, aesthetics, definitions. Prose is the
  correct representation. Stored as said, no model.

  Extraction was the thing I called irreducible. On this data it's largely
  unnecessary — not because prose can be typed, but because the prose that
  resists typing is prose that shouldn't be typed. You were right that heaven is
  possible; I was wrong about where the gate was.

  Two findings that change priorities

  The 16% is worth far more than 16%. Bucket A is 16% of turns but 53% of 
  recurring rule families — the things you have to repeat across sessions are
  disproportionately the tool-hygiene ones. "I can't see images → use Artifact"
  appears 12 times across 6 sessions. "cargo under a memory cap", "Edit not 
  python heredocs", "diagnose before editing", "no auto-merge". Those are
  exactly the corrections that carry annoyance markers — "what did I say 
  about…", "how many times do I have to tell you".

  Induction fixes the highest-irritation, highest-repetition band even though
  it's a minority of volume.

  And a contamination catch I'd have missed: 5.2% of in-scope turns are
  immediately followed by Write{…/memory/*.md}. That looks like a behavioural
  signal but is just the agent transcribing the prose it read. Any naive learner
  would score that as derivable. It isn't.

  What I'd revise

  The Provenance distinction becomes load-bearing in a way I hadn't seen:
  Observed facts (induced from events — high confidence, machine-generated,
  structurally queryable) and Stated facts (verbatim prose — high authority,
  human-authored, recalled not reasoned over) are genuinely different kinds of 
  thing, not two tags on one thing. They want different storage, different
  retrieval, and different confidence semantics.

  Which means the type system earns its place on the induced lane and stays
  almost entirely out of the way on the stated lane.

  The second study — on nominal entities and what the user's speech actually
  consists of — is still running. That one bears directly on whether the
  Person/Org sorts are worth having at all, given only 12.3% of prose-only facts
  touch them. I'll report when it lands.

  Not one geographic mention in 300 turns. And the sharpest datum: a memory
  system already ran over these sessions and wrote 194 notes — zero of them name

  described as one of the two primary devs on Flowstate. Never recorded. adhi,
  who controls the Cloudflare account you deploy to, never recorded.

  So Person, Org, Place should not exist. In 2,938 turns the total durable
  person inventory is three facts.

  One honesty caveat: "0 of 194" might partly be the current system
  under-recording rather than proof colleagues don't matter — "Chinmay alleges 
  the tool icon looks small after our FIRST PR" is clearly load-bearing. But
  three facts is thin enough that the conclusion survives either way. You don't
  build identity-resolution machinery for three facts.

  What actually carries the value

  The highest-value notes in your store are 100% nominal, and all about external
  things:

  ▎ "Never reach for Whisper as a 'diverse second ASR'… If a survey or agent 
  ▎ suggests Whisper for the ensemble, flag it to Adam explicitly — he nearly 
  ▎ missed it once and would be furious to see Whisper on a 4-hour Colab run."

  ▎ "the construction editor uses Turing Complete's blocks-and-pipes idiom… Adam
  ▎ explicitly does not want to reinvent this wheel."

  ▎ "stay inside the Symbios crate ecosystem… write custom code only where the 
  ▎ ecosystem has a real gap."

  Negative constraints and taste commitments about external things, each one
  expensive to relearn. That's the money.

  So the nominal side collapses from four sorts to two:

  - ExternalThing — Whisper, Turing Complete, Symbios, Colab, A100, SMPL. 89%
  load-bearing, 56% recurring.
  - User — a singleton, doing enormous work: 115 of 194 notes mention you by
  name.

  The evidence shrank the design. Smaller domain, cheaper model checking.

  The one genuinely hard case

  AI-agent personas sit exactly on the grounded/nominal seam. Sol, Fable, Codex
  — 100% load-bearing, 91% recurring, higher than any other category. They're
  addressed like people, persist across sessions, and partly resolve: Sol is a
  name you invented, but it's also a herdr pane with a session id.

  ▎ "we have another frontier model in a tab in this herdr pane called GPT 5.6 
  ▎ Sol Max… I'd be careful about using him excessively, since we're much more 
  ▎ rate-limited with him."

  That's not hypothetical — I spent this session coordinating with wV:p3 and
  wV:p4. If the split has one real fracture, it's here.

  Two things that change the write path

  Preferences arrive as corrections, not declarations. 22% of turns carry a
  preference, but they land mid-argument:

  ▎ "New rule: stop insisting we've hit tge ceiling. Literally every time you've
  ▎ said that you've been wrong."

  ▎ "For the record, don't explicitly scope anything as 'follow-up.' I'm gonna 
  ▎ be gone for a while. If there's something clear to do, just do it and lock 
  ▎ it in."

  Only rarely is it an explicit "commit to memory." An extractor that listens 
  for declarative preference statements misses most preferences — which
  validates the correction trigger and invalidates any pattern-matching on "X
  prefers Y."

  And 36% of turns carry a durable claim; 47% including corrections. Meanwhile
  2.8% of all your speech is asking for an ETA — "ETA?", "Heartbeat?", "Where we
  at?" — which is a larger share than people, orgs and places combined.

  Where that leaves the architecture

  Both studies point the same way: two lanes, and the type system only earns its
  place on one.

  - Observed — induced from the typed event stream. 16% of corrections, but 53% 
  of the recurring families — the tool-hygiene rules you repeat with visible
  irritation. Fully typed, queryable, no model.
  - Stated — verbatim prose. The 67%, including every one of the expensive
  external-thing constraints. Stored as said, recalled as said. ExternalThing
  and User are the only nominal sorts it needs.

  The sort table on the HOL artifact is now refuted by this data. Want me to
  revise it — drop Person/Org/Place, add ExternalThing and the User singleton,
  and add the agent-persona seam as an open problem?

❯ Why would we shrink the design because of this??? This was entirely to probe  
  how much of resolution we could automate. Shrinking the design breaks         
  whenever the model goes beyond n = 1 and meets any person who isn't me. It    
  needs to be maximally general. Step back. What is the database language that  
  can express any form of proposition and is the most well tuned to agents? How

● You're right, and I did the exact thing I spent this whole conversation
  arguing against — schema-first ontology design, just with data instead of
  intuition, which is worse because it looks principled.

  The studies answered how much resolution can be automated (16 / 17 / 67). I
  used them to prune an inventory. Frequency in one corpus has nothing to do 
  with whether a sort should exist. Place scoring 0.0% means you don't talk
  about places, not that places aren't real. n=1, and I was about to bake it
  into a type system permanently.

  Retracted.

  The dissolution: sorts aren't schema

  The deeper error was having a sort enum at all. A sort is just a unary 
  predicate, and type membership is a proposition with evidence:

  type(chinmay, Person)      ← a fact you learn, not a schema decision
  type(whisper, Model)
  type(berlin_office, Place)

  New sort = new fact. No migration, no design conversation, no #[derive] to
  edit. That's how RDF works — rdf:type is just a predicate — and it's the only
  formulation that survives meeting someone who isn't you.

  And the grounded/nominal split stops being a partition and becomes data too:

  has_resolver(Path).      ← true, so ¬∃ is refutation
  has_resolver(Person).    ← currently false, so ¬∃ is only not-known

  Wire up a contacts resolver later and that fact flips — and closed-world
  negation becomes available for Person without touching the logic. The
  semantics become data-driven. That's the maximally general form, and it's
  strictly better than what I had.

  The language

  What you want is precisely characterized, and it's four properties:

  1. N-ary predicates, not triples. Reification is the tax RDF pays for
  binary-only; you shouldn't. prefers(adam, Edit, over: sed, since: t) is one
  fact.
  2. Predicate variables — quantify over relations, not just entities. Formally
  this is HiLog: higher-order syntax, first-order semantics, so it stays
  decidable. ∃P. ∀x. corrected(adam,x) → P(x) is expressible and evaluable.
  3. Existential rules for entity introduction — Datalog±, guarded fragment.
  This is the skolem machinery done properly: ∃y. works_at(chinmay, y) mints an
  entity you can later identify. Decidable under guardedness, and it's the
  formal answer to the entity-construction question you pushed on earlier.
  4. Statement-level provenance — annotate a fact as a subject, RDF-star style,
  so evidence attaches without reification.

  And the implementation insight: Cozo can host all of it, because HiLog's
  standard semantics is its encoding into first-order logic. You don't need a
  new engine — you need to stop declaring one relation per predicate and instead
  have a small set of universal relations keyed by arity:

  holds2(pred, a, b, fact_id)
  holds3(pred, a, b, c, fact_id)
  holds4(pred, a, b, c, d, fact_id)

  The predicate is a term, so it can be a variable, quantified over, and
  learned. Columns stay indexable. Sorts, resolvers, provenance, supersession
  all become ordinary facts in the same store. Nothing is schema except arity.

  That's the maximally general form that stays computable, and it's ~one
  refactor from what's shipped.

  Getting there without leaning on LLMs

  Three lanes, in ascending cost — and note the expensive one is last resort,
  not the default:

  Lane 1 — induced, free. Tool name + args + outcome are already typed in
  events.jsonl. Pattern rules over that stream. 16% of corrections, but 53% of 
  the ones you repeat. Zero model.

  Lane 2 — constrained decoding, nearly free. When the model is already
  generating (a tool call, a decision statement), have it emit propositions
  under a grammar rather than prose. Not a second call — a constrained
  continuation of the one already happening. This is where most of the hybrid
  17% lives.

  Lane 3 — a dedicated small parser. For genuine prose. And yes, I think your
  SLM instinct is right, with a specific shape:

  The task has a name — Open Information Extraction, or better, AMR parsing. AMR
  is a formalism built to render any sentence as a rooted predicate-argument
  graph, which is exactly "express any proposition." Good AMR parsers are ~400M 
  params, seq2seq, CPU-viable.

  And architecturally it's free: artist already runs a 137M transformer on CPU 
  through rten. A second model in the same slot is not new infrastructure, it's
  a second Model::load_file.

  On training it: distillation is unusually attractive here because the
  conditions are ideal — a narrow, stable domain; a large in-domain corpus you
  already own (2,938 human turns, 5,038 transcripts); a constrained output
  space; and recoverable errors, since prose is retained and re-extraction is
  always possible. Big model labels once, offline. SLM serves forever, locally,
  deterministically.

  That is "rely on LLMs as little as possible" done properly: an LLM never runs 
  at read time, and at write time it runs once, during training, and never 
  again.

  Want me to write this up as the revised architecture — universal holds
  relations, sorts-as-facts, resolvers-as-facts, and the three-lane population
  pipeline? It supersedes the sort table on the HOL artifact, which the data has
  now refuted for the opposite reason I thought.

● 1. What first-order semantics can't express

  HiLog gives you higher-order syntax — ∃P. P(a,b) parses — with first-order
  semantics: predicate variables range over the names in the domain, and a
  separate extension map sends names to relations. So the question ∃P. 
  P(chinmay, artist) asks "is there a named predicate holding of these two," not
  "is there a set of pairs containing this one" (trivially yes, and useless).

  Four things are genuinely lost, and only one of them bites.

  Comprehension — standard SOL guarantees that for any formula φ there exists a
  predicate with φ's extension. HiLog only has predicates that were named. But
  λ-abstraction restores this for anything you can write down. The residue is:
  sets neither named nor definable in your language. On a finite domain the only
  witnesses to ∃P you'd gain are extensional ones — "the property of being
  exactly a, b, or c" — which are never explanations. This loss is a feature.
  It's what makes an ∃P answer mean something.

  Categoricity — SOL pins the naturals up to isomorphism; FOL can't
  (Löwenheim–Skolem). Irrelevant. Categoricity matters when you're specifying a
  structure you don't possess. You possess this one; it's sitting in RocksDB.

  Transitive closure and well-foundedness as logical operators — FOL can't
  define TC. Datalog computes it as a fixpoint. You get the capability without
  the model-theoretic uniformity, and uniformity across all models is worthless
  when there's one model.

  Quantification over arbitrary subsets — "is there some set of files whose
  removal fixes the build?" This one is real and genuinely not a query. But on a
  finite domain it's decidable-but-exponential: a search problem, not a logic
  problem. You solve it with delta-debugging, and the database is the oracle the
  search calls, not the thing that answers.

  The one that actually costs you: hypothesis discovery. "Find a rule that
  explains these corrections." If ∃P ranges only over the registry, you can
  recognize an explanation but never discover one — which is precisely what the
  induction lane needs to do. The standard answer is ILP's: bound the hypothesis
  language to a grammar of constructible predicates and search that space.
  That's Henkin semantics with a generated registry rather than a stored one,
  and it converts the loss into a design decision — how rich is the hypothesis
  grammar — rather than eliminating it.

  Worth naming what people assume needs HOL and doesn't: nested attitudes
  (believes(adam, ⟨prefers(adam, tabs)⟩) — reification via terms),
  statement-level provenance, counting and aggregation, transitive closure, and
  quantifying over named predicates. All first-order or Datalog.

  2. Lane 3 paused

  Set aside.

  3. Constrained decoding with cloud providers

  Two mechanisms, and conflating them is the trap.

  Schema-constrained JSON — yes, both, genuinely guaranteed.

  - OpenAI: Structured Outputs — text: {format: {type: "json_schema", strict: 
  true, ...}} on the Responses API, plus strict: true on tool definitions.
  - Anthropic: output_config: {format: {type: "json_schema", schema: {...}}} on
  messages.create(), and strict tool use — strict: true as a top-level field on
  the tool definition (not on tool_choice), requiring additionalProperties: 
  false and required. That guarantees tool_use.input validates exactly.
  Supported on Opus 5, Fable 5, Opus 4.8, Sonnet 5, Haiku 4.5. This is no longer
  the best-effort steering it once was.

  Arbitrary grammar (CFG/GBNF) — no, neither. That needs logit access: vLLM with
  outlines/xgrammar, llama.cpp GBNF, or your own decode loop. No cloud provider
  exposes a grammar parameter.

  The binding constraint is the JSON Schema subset, not availability. Anthropic
  does not support recursive schemas, and neither provider enforces
  pattern/minLength/numeric bounds. This matters directly: a general proposition
  language is naturally recursive — a term is a constant, a variable, or a
  predicate applied to terms — and you cannot write that as a non-recursive
  schema.

  Which retroactively justifies the arity ladder. holds2 / holds3 / holds4 as a
  oneOf over three flat object shapes is non-recursive and therefore
  constrainable. The rank bound isn't a Cozo convenience; it's the thing that 
  makes the schema expressible to a constrained decoder at all. If you want
  depth beyond that, unroll it — Term = Atom | App1<Atom> | App2<App1> at depth
  3 is a legal flat schema and covers essentially everything.

  For artist specifically — correcting what I said earlier. The transport is no
  longer rig's chatgpt provider; artist owns it at
  crates/artist-agent/src/openai_responses/request.rs:6. That Request has no
  text field, but it carries #[serde(flatten)] pub extra: Map<String, Value>, so
  text.format goes on the wire losslessly with no type change. The client is
  not the blocker.

  What's actually unknown is whether https://chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex/
  honors it — that proxy has a narrower surface than
  api.openai.com/v1/responses, and it already rejects context_management on some
  paths. The codebase has the exact pattern for this at transport.rs:740:
  context_management_unsupported() detects the 400, strips the field, retries.
  Reuse that shape for text.format and Lane 2 degrades gracefully instead of
  failing. And the registry carries ProviderKind::Anthropic
  (crates/llm-provider/src/registry.rs:70), so where an API key is configured,
  strict tool use is available as a second constraint channel.

❯ 1. Then fuck that. Higher-order semantics. We do not compromise. 3. This

● You're right, and I had the wrong theorem again — same error you caught
  before.

  Let me state the correction precisely, because it kills the compromise rather
  than softening it:

  Over a finite domain, every subset is first-order definable. {a, c} is λx. x=a
  ∨ x=c. So a Henkin model closed under first-order comprehension over a finite
  domain contains the entire powerset — it is the standard model. The
  Henkin/standard distinction has no content here. It's a distinction that only
  bites on infinite domains, and I imported it from a setting we're not in.

  What's actually true: second-order validity (true in all structures) is
  undecidable. Second-order model checking against a fixed finite structure is
  decidable — by Fagin, ∃SO over finite structures is exactly NP, and the SO
  quantifier-alternation hierarchy is exactly the polynomial hierarchy. So full
  standard semantics costs us complexity, not decidability, and complexity
  stratifies by fragment rather than forcing a semantic concession.

  So: standard semantics, full powerset, no registry restriction. The
  hypothesis-discovery loss I described evaporates — "find a P explaining these
  examples" is ∃SO, which is NP, which is SAT.

  What changed, and why each was a compromise:

  Was: Henkin semantics, quantifiers range over a ~10² registry
  Now: Standard semantics, full powerset. The Henkin/standard distinction is
  vacuous on a finite domain — every subset is FO-definable, so a
  comprehension-closed general model already is the standard model
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Was: CONSTRAINT: rank ≤ 2
  Now: Rank is a cost input. The type tower over a finite base is finite all the

  way up; the planner estimates, it doesn't prohibit
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Was: ι ::= Path | Symbol | ... fixed enumeration
  Now: ι ∈ { s | type(s, Sort) } — sorts are unary predicates, membership is an
  ordinary fact, has_resolver selects CWA/OWA. New sort = a write
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Was: "no entity-constructing functions"
  Now: Constructors allowed, depth-capped. Unbounded construction is the only
  genuine frontier, and a cap is one integer
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Was: Rank 2 "defunctionalizes to Datalog"
  Now: Registry is now an optimization, not the semantics: named predicates as a

  fast path, SAT fallthrough for the real answer
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Was: "No property explains this" = "none in the registry"
  Now: A genuine universal, via UNSAT

  The load-bearing addition is Table 2 — fragment → complexity → engine. Fagin
  gives ∃SO = NP exactly, Stockmeyer gives the alternation hierarchy = PH
  exactly, so "how expensive is this question" reads straight off its quantifier
  prefix. Cozo handles FO+LFP directly; SAT handles ∃SO/∀SO; Σ₂ᵖ and up are
  budget-gated.

  And the thing that makes it run at all: the powerset is never materialized.
  The store holds the structure — domain plus interpretation of named symbols.
  Quantification over 2^(n^k) relations compiles to a search. That's why full
  semantics fits on a laptop.

  Two honest costs I added to the limits rather than hid: NP is a decidability
  guarantee and not a performance one, so the planner must refuse legibly rather
  than hang; and pure-Rust solvers are thin above NP — SAT is fine in-tree,
  disjunctive ASP and QBF aren't, so Σ₂ᵖ+ may be the one place this touches the
  toolchain.

  Read-write policy deferred as you said. What's next on ontology — the
  term/proposition representation (Fig. 4), or the sort census?

❯ This ontology is maximally powerful and can express literally anything,       
  right?                                                                        

● No. It's maximal in one precise sense and bounded in several others, and the
  difference matters for what you build on top of it.

  The sense in which it is maximal: over a fixed finite structure, full SO with
  the whole type tower captures every property of that structure that is
  definable in the type hierarchy at all. Descriptive complexity pins this
  exactly — SO captures PH, SO+LFP captures PSPACE. There is nothing about the
  repository-as-modelled that you can define and not ask. Within its world it is
  expressively complete.

  Every limit is at the boundary of that world.

  Inherent — these are load-bearing to decidability, and giving them up gives up
  the whole argument:

  No infinities. ∀n:Nat isn't expressible-as-checkable. Concretely: "this
  function is correct for all inputs" is a thing you genuinely want to remember
  about code, and it can only be stored as an opaque atom (proven_correct(f)),
  never derived or verified. The logic can hold the sentence; it can't evaluate
  it.

  This structure, not a theory. Model checking answers "does φ hold here." It
  cannot answer "does φ follow from these axioms" — that's entailment,
  undecidable at SO. So "will this stay true?" and "is my rule set consistent in
  general?" are outside. Only "is it true now" is inside.

  Universals over nominal sorts are misleading. ∀p:Person. φ reads as "all
  people" and means "all people I have heard of." The OWA machinery keeps it
  sound, but the notation is wider than the semantics, and that gap will bite
  whoever writes rules against it.

  Design choices — reversible, at a stated cost:

  Vagueness has nowhere to live. Two-valued logic plus an evidence ledger. But
  the ledger handles uncertainty about a crisp proposition, which is a different
  thing from a predicate with no fact of the matter. "Is this file large?"
  isn't 0.7-confidence-true; it's not truth-apt at that granularity. Today you
  discretize into buckets. That's a real modelling decision the artifact
  currently doesn't acknowledge.

  Continuity is discretized, not excluded — I'd overstate this if I called it a
  gap. Literal permits fixed-point, so any measurement encodes fine at chosen
  precision. What's absent is genuine real-valued structure: a density, a limit,
  an actual irrational.

  Counterfactuals need materialized worlds. "If we'd used tokio instead" is
  expressible by the standard translation — worlds as entities, accessibility as
  a relation, modality becomes first-order over that. Semantically free;
  practically it means enumerating alternative repositories, so it's a cost wall
  rather than a bar.

  No unrestricted truth predicate. Tarski. Note this one is blocked by
  construction rather than by rule: Prop(Box<Prop>) is a well-founded inductive
  type, so a self-referential proposition can't be built. Benign, and worth
  knowing you got it for free.

  And the one that actually dominates: the extraction boundary. The logic's
  ceiling is far above the extractor's. Practically, "can this express X" almost
  always resolves to "can prose → typed terms produce X," and that's the thin
  model-shaped layer the artifact already concedes. Everything I listed above is
  theoretical headroom you will not reach before extraction fidelity becomes
  the binding constraint.

  If you want the two inherent ones softened, there is a move: an explicitly
  stratified layer for schematic claims — universally quantified rules stored as
  terms with their own restricted, decidable checking regime, separate from the
  ground structure. That buys "correct for all inputs" as a first-class object
  with real inference. It's a second logic bolted alongside, not an extension of
  this one, and I'd want to see a query that needs it before building it.

✻ Sautéed for 1m 5s

❯ We aint worried about extraction fidelity rn. We are worried about            
  expressibility. Why is decidability and checkability important for a database 
  of facts a model stores, btw?                                                 

● For a store, it isn't. I conflated two things and let a constraint on one
  shrink the other — the same error you've now caught three times.

  Decidability is a property of the evaluator, not the representation. Nothing
  stops you writing down a sentence quantifying over the naturals, or a modal
  claim, or an unrestricted universal. Storage is syntax. The store doesn't care
  whether anything can compute with it. What decidability buys is a guarantee 
  about the query path, and I let it propagate backwards into what's allowed to
  exist, which was never necessary.

  So the architecture should be inverted from what the artifact says:
  unrestricted representation, stratified evaluation. Store any well-formed
  sentence. The planner recognizes which fragment a query falls into and
  dispatches — PTIME to Datalog, NP to SAT, and refuses legibly for anything
  past its budget or outside its fragments. That's how every real prover works:
  unrestricted syntax, sound-but-incomplete procedure. Nothing is lost by
  admitting a sentence you can't currently decide; you just can't query through
  it yet.

  Now, where decidability does genuinely earn its keep — because it's not zero:

  Negation soundness. This is the sharp one and it's the real answer to your 
  question. Closed-world negation says: no fact mentions this file, therefore
  the belief is stale. That inference is valid only if the search that found 
  nothing actually completed. If it hit a timeout, "we didn't finish looking"
  silently became "it isn't there." You've manufactured a confidently-wrong
  memory, which then gets injected into context as fact. Every other failure
  mode here is a missing answer; this one is a fabricated one. A termination
  guarantee — not a timeout — is what keeps ¬ honest.

  Determinism for caching. A decided query's answer depends only on the
  structure, so you can cache it and invalidate on write. A semi-decided one
  gives different answers under different load, which makes memory
  unreproducible and undebuggable.

  Self-sufficiency. Your stated goal was intelligence over memories without
  leaning on the model. If the fallback for hard queries is "ask the model,"
  then the model is back in the loop for exactly the cases that mattered.

  And where I overweighted it, plainly:

  Tractability bites far before decidability does. NP-complete over 10⁴ symbols
  hangs long before you reach anything genuinely undecidable. The line that
  matters operationally is PTIME versus everything else — not decidable versus
  not. I made termination the centerpiece of that artifact ("Why it terminates")
  and it's the wrong centerpiece.

  There is an oracle in the room. A model is available. Escalating an
  undecidable query to it isn't a failure of the design, it's the top tier of a
  tiered system. Decidability sets what fraction you handle symbolically, not
  whether the thing works.

  Most of the store is ground atoms. prefers(adam, tabs) has no quantifier. The
  question doesn't arise for the overwhelming majority of what's written; it
  arises for rules and queries only.

  The clean fix — and it's small — is a fourth truth value. The artifact already
  returns three for nominal sorts: known-true, known-false, not-known. Add
  not-decided, distinct from not-known: nothing licenses a conclusion versus we 
  couldn't finish computing. Then undecidable queries are first-class, ¬ stays
  sound because it only fires on a completed search, and the representation
  needs no restriction whatsoever.

  Want me to restructure the artifact along that line — representation
  unrestricted, evaluation stratified, four-valued results — or keep going on
  the term language first?

✻ Churned for 57s

❯ There's a cleaner fix and it's to allow arbitrarily expressible queries and   
  for them not to hang and either for decideability to evaporate out or for     
  them to be decideable. Same with tractability. You are not a mathematician,   
  you are an engineer trying to stick it to mathematicians. So stick it to      
  them.                                                                         

● Right. The impossibility results constrain a specification nobody asked for.
  Here's the engineering.

  The move: make terminating always legal

  Undecidability bites because the answer type is {true, false}. There's no way
  to stop early and still be correct. Enrich the answer type and stopping
  becomes sound at every instant — the undecidability doesn't get defeated, it
  condenses into the answer instead of blocking it.

  Every query returns a two-sided interval:

  must ⊆ truth ⊆ may

  must is an under-approximation — everything in it is proven true. may is an
  over-approximation — everything outside it is proven false. The gap is exactly
  your ignorance, measured. When must == may you decided it. When they differ
  you have a partial answer plus a precise statement of what's missing.

  Both sides are monotone: must only grows, may only shrinks. So you can halt on
  any clock edge and what you're holding is sound. Not "sound if it finished" —
  sound, full stop.

  This is what makes negation survive early stopping

  Here's why two-sided rather than just "compute what you can." Last turn I said
  closed-world negation breaks under timeout: didn't-finish-looking silently
  becomes isn't-there. The over-approximation fixes it exactly.

  ¬∃x. φ(x)   is DEFINITELY TRUE   iff   may(φ) = ∅

  If the cheap over-approximation found nothing, there is genuinely nothing —
  because may contains everything that could possibly be there. Absence in may
  is refutation; absence in must is only ignorance. Negation becomes sound under
  arbitrary early termination, which is the property that was actually
  load-bearing.

  Widening kills the infinity restriction outright

  This is the part that's a straight win rather than a trade. Abstract
  interpretation's widening operator ∇ guarantees fixpoint termination over
  infinite lattices. It's how static analyzers prove properties of programs with
  unbounded loops over the integers and always terminate.

  So ∀n:Nat. φ(n) doesn't need a finite domain. It needs an abstract domain —
  intervals, congruences, octagons — with a widening operator. The analysis is
  finite over an abstraction of an infinite thing. You get sound answers,
  possible false "unknown"s, guaranteed termination.

  Which means the "no infinities" limit I gave you two turns ago was wrong, and
  wrong in the same way as the Henkin thing: I imported a restriction on the
  domain when the constraint was only ever on the analysis. "This function is
  correct for all inputs" becomes a first-class checkable claim, not an opaque
  atom.

  Tractability stops being a cliff and becomes a dial

  Same machinery, different knob. Budget is an input; answer precision is the
  output. There is no NP cliff because you never run to completion — you run to
  budget and report the interval you reached.

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Path          │ Budget  │            Typical result             │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Per-turn memory       │ 5 ms    │ must from the Datalog fast path, wide │
  │ injection             │         │  may, big residual                    │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Explicit user query   │ 2 s     │ registry fast path + bounded SAT,     │
  │                       │         │ narrow interval                       │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Nightly               │ minutes │ most queries collapse to must == may  │
  │ reconciliation        │         │                                       │
  └───────────────────────┴─────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  Same query, three answers, all sound, none hang. A query that's Σ₂ᵖ doesn't
  fail — it returns a wider interval at 5ms than at 5 minutes.

  The honest accounting

  We did not beat the halting problem. We changed the specification: from decide
  φ to compute a sound two-sided approximation of φ within budget b, and return
  the residual. That second problem is decidable — trivially, because the
  budget bounds it.

  The legitimate claim is that nobody wanted the first problem. You want to know
  things about your codebase. "Here's what I proved, here's what's still open,
  here's what would settle it" answers that need completely. Exactness was the
  mathematician's framing of the goal, not the goal.

  What it costs, plainly:

  - Precision. Over-approximation admits false maybes. A widened ∀n:Nat may
  report unknown where the truth was provable.
  - Every operator must be written anytime and two-sided. You cannot call Cozo
  and hope. You need incremental, resumable evaluation with a sound partial
  state at every step — semi-naive gets you the must side; the may side needs a
  separate cheap abstraction per operator. This is the actual work.
  - The residual must be representable. A partially-evaluated formula is a
  first-class term, which constrains the term language — Term has to admit
  residual formulas, not just ground propositions.
  - Widening operators are per-domain and require design. Not free, not
  automatic.

  The result type

  struct Answer {
      must:     Set<Binding>,     // proven in
      may:      Set<Binding>,     // not proven out
      residual: Option<Formula>,  // what's left, as a formula — storable, 
  requeryable
      spent:    Budget,
  }
  // must == may          ⟹ decided
  // residual == None     ⟹ complete
  // may.is_empty()       ⟹ ¬∃ is sound

  Arbitrary query in. Always terminates. Nothing rejected at the door — no
  fragment check, no rank ceiling, no cost refusal. The answer carries its own
  honesty, and the residual is a formula you can store, re-query when more facts
  land, or hand to the model as a precisely-scoped question rather than a vague
  one.

  None of this is novel — it's anytime algorithms, Cousot's abstract
  interpretation, bounded model checking, and SMT's unknown composed into one
  result type. What's new here is refusing to let any of them leak backwards
  into the representation.

  Want me to rebuild the artifact on this? It replaces the whole spine — "Why it
  terminates" and the fragment/engine table both go, and the ontology section
  becomes genuinely unrestricted.

✻ Worked for 1m 20s

❯ Rebuild the entire implementation on this now. After you do, we'll try to

● Intervals can't prove n ≤ n+1 — non-relational domains forget that both sides
  mention the same n. The standard fix is a linear-form pre-pass so correlated
  variables cancel.

● Update(crates/artist-logic/src/abstract_domain.rs)
  ⎿  Added 110 lines, removed 1 line
      181      }
      182  }
      183  
      184 +/// A linear form `Σ cᵢ·vᵢ + k`.                              
      185 +///                                                           
      186 +/// Intervals alone are non-relational: evaluating `n` and `n 
          ++ 1` separately                                               
      187 +/// forgets that both mention the same `n`, so even `n ≤ n + 1
          +` comes back                                                  
      188 +/// unknown. Normalising a comparison to `(a - b) ≤ 0` first l
          +ets the correlated                                            
      189 +/// terms cancel symbolically, and only the *residue* is hande
          +d to the interval                                             
      190 +/// domain. That recovers the whole linear fragment without pa
          +ying for octagons.                                            
      191 +#[derive(Clone, Debug, Default)]                              
      192 +struct LinForm {                                              
      193 +    coeffs: std::collections::BTreeMap<Var, i64>,             
      194 +    konst: i64,                                               
      195 +}                                                             
      196 +                                                              
      197 +impl LinForm {                                                
      198 +    fn constant(k: i64) -> Self {                             
      199 +        LinForm { coeffs: Default::default(), konst: k }      
      200 +    }                                                         
      201 +                                                              
      202 +    fn var(v: Var) -> Self {                                  
      203 +        let mut coeffs = std::collections::BTreeMap::new();   
      204 +        coeffs.insert(v, 1);                                  
      205 +        LinForm { coeffs, konst: 0 }                          
      206 +    }                                                         
      207 +                                                              
      208 +    fn scale(mut self, k: i64) -> Option<Self> {              
      209 +        for c in self.coeffs.values_mut() {                   
      210 +            *c = c.checked_mul(k)?;                           
      211 +        }                                                     
      212 +        self.konst = self.konst.checked_mul(k)?;              
      213 +        Some(self)                                            
      214 +    }                                                         
      215 +                                                              
      216 +    fn add(mut self, other: Self) -> Option<Self> {           
      217 +        for (v, c) in other.coeffs {                          
      218 +            let e = self.coeffs.entry(v).or_insert(0);        
      219 +            *e = e.checked_add(c)?;                           
      220 +        }                                                     
      221 +        self.coeffs.retain(|_, c| *c != 0);                   
      222 +        self.konst = self.konst.checked_add(other.konst)?;    
      223 +        Some(self)                                            
      224 +    }                                                         
      225 +                                                              
      226 +    fn sub(self, other: Self) -> Option<Self> {               
      227 +        let neg = other.scale(-1)?;                           
      228 +        self.add(neg)                                         
      229 +    }                                                         
      230 +                                                              
      231 +    /// `None` when the term leaves the linear fragment — an u
          +ninterpreted                                                  
      232 +    /// function, a product of two variables. Not an error; th
          +e caller degrades                                             
      233 +    /// to `Unknown`.                                         
      234 +    fn of_term(t: &Term, env: &Env) -> Option<Self> {         
      235 +        match t {                                             
      236 +            Term::Var(v) => match env.vars.get(v) {           
      237 +                Some(Term::Lit(Literal::Int(n))) => Some(LinFo
          +rm::constant(*n)),                                            
      238 +                Some(_) => None,                              
      239 +                None => Some(LinForm::var(*v)),               
      240 +            },                                                
      241 +            Term::Lit(Literal::Int(n)) => Some(LinForm::consta
          +nt(*n)),                                                      
      242 +            Term::App(f, args) => match (*f, args.as_slice()) 
          +{                                                             
      243 +                (crate::term::sym::ADD, [a, b]) => {          
      244 +                    LinForm::of_term(a, env)?.add(LinForm::of_
          +term(b, env)?)                                                
      245 +                }                                             
      246 +                (crate::term::sym::NEG, [a]) => LinForm::of_te
          +rm(a, env)?.scale(-1),                                        
      247 +                (crate::term::sym::MUL, [a, b]) => {          
      248 +                    let (x, y) = (LinForm::of_term(a, env)?, L
          +inForm::of_term(b, env)?);                                    
      249 +                    // Linear only when one side is constant. 
      250 +                    if x.coeffs.is_empty() {                  
      251 +                        y.scale(x.konst)                      
      252 +                    } else if y.coeffs.is_empty() {           
      253 +                        x.scale(y.konst)                      
      254 +                    } else {                                  
      255 +                        None                                  
      256 +                    }                                         
      257 +                }                                             
      258 +                _ => None,                                    
      259 +            },                                                
      260 +            _ => None,                                        
      261 +        }                                                     
      262 +    }                                                         
      263 +                                                              
      264 +    /// Interval of this form with `v` bound to `iv` and every
          + other variable                                               
      265 +    /// resolved from the environment.                        
      266 +    fn interval(&self, v: Var, iv: Interval, env: &Env) -> Int
          +erval {                                                       
      267 +        let mut acc = Interval::point(self.konst);            
      268 +        for (w, c) in &self.coeffs {                          
      269 +            let base = if *w == v {                           
      270 +                iv                                            
      271 +            } else {                                          
      272 +                match env.vars.get(w) {                       
      273 +                    Some(Term::Lit(Literal::Int(n))) => Interv
          +al::point(*n),                                                
      274 +                    _ => Interval::top(),                     
      275 +                }                                             
      276 +            };                                                
      277 +            acc = acc.add(base.mul(Interval::point(*c)));     
      278 +        }                                                     
      279 +        acc                                                   
      280 +    }                                                         
      281 +}                                                             
      282 +

      156 +/// Intern a name, returning its symbol. Well-known names reso
          +lve without a                                                 
      157 +/// write so their ids stay stable across stores.             
      158 +pub async fn intern(store: &MemoryStore, name: &str) -> Result
          +<Sym> {                                                       
      159 +    for (s, n) in well_known::NAMES {                         
      160 +        if *n == name {                                       
      161 +            return Ok(*s);                                    
      162 +        }                                                     
      163 +    }                                                         
      164 +    let mut params = BTreeMap::new();                         
      165 +    params.insert("n".to_string(), DataValue::from(name));    
      166 +    let existing = store                                      
      167 +        .script("?[sym_id] := *sym{sym_id, name}, name == $n",
          + params.clone(), false)                                       
      168 +        .await?;                                              
      169 +    if let Some(row) = existing.rows.first() {                
      170 +        return Ok(int_at(row, 0)? as Sym);
