● Built and green. 34 tests pass, workspace checks clean.

  New crate: artist-logic

  Storage-independent, so the semantics are testable without RocksDB.

  File: term.rs
  What's in it: Unrestricted grammar. No rank ceiling, no arity bound, sorts
  aren't in the syntax (ι ∈ { s | type(s, Sort) }). Quote is inductive, so the
  liar is blocked by construction rather than by a stratification rule.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: answer.rs
  What's in it: The interval. must ⊆ truth ⊆ may, may: None = no upper bound
  established, plus Budget in steps not wall-clock — a query that answers
  differently under load can't be cached.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: eval.rs
  What's in it: Kleene-strong 3-valued evaluator. Short-circuits, second-order
  search over the full powerset with a named-predicate fast path, residual =
  the unexamined tail as a formula.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: abstract_domain.rs
  What's in it: Intervals + widening + a linear-form normaliser.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: structure.rs
  What's in it: The seam — four methods, plus MapStructure for tests.

  The load-bearing detail

  Answer::refuted() is matches!(may, Some(s) if s.is_empty()) — an upper bound
  established and empty. That single predicate is what makes closed-world
  negation survive interruption, and truncated_scan_never_refutes pins it: same
  query, 2 steps of budget, asserts !refuted(), may.is_none(), and partial.must 
  ⊆ complete.must.

  One thing didn't work first try

  ∀n:Nat. n ≤ n+1 came back Unknown. Intervals are non-relational — evaluating n
  and n+1 separately forgets they're the same n. Fixed by normalising to (a−b) 
  ≤ 0 as a linear form first, so correlated variables cancel symbolically and
  only the residue reaches the interval domain. Recovers the whole linear
  fragment without paying for octagons.

  Infinity results

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Query      │                        Result                         │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ∀n:Nat. n ≤ n+1 │ True — tail closed by widening                        │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ∀n:Nat. n ≤ 10  │ False — concrete counterexample, over an infinite     │
  │                 │ domain                                                │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ∀n:Nat.         │ Unknown + the formula back intact                     │
  │ halts(n)        │                                                       │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  All terminate well inside budget. "No infinities" is off the limits list.

  Storage

  stmt + arg, not a holds2/3/4 ladder — a ladder would put a bound in storage
  the logic doesn't have. Verified with a 7-ary statement. Sorts and
  closed-world status are derived from facts:
  sorts_and_closure_are_facts_not_schema shows a negation flip from Unknown to
  True after writing one has_resolver statement. RelationalView snapshots,
  because bounds computed over a shifting structure don't compose.

  Artifact updated — spine replaced, and the limits now say what actually bites:
  precision is what you pay, every operator must be hand-written two-sided, and
  the abstract domain only covers the linear fragment.

  Ready for the halting-problem push. My read on where the real headroom is: the
  residual is currently just the unexamined tail, but if it were simplified
  against what was learned, a query could return strictly smaller each time it's
  re-asked — which turns budget-exhaustion into progress rather than a stall.

❯ Explain what you mean by that last paragraph. I'm clever, but not smart lol.  
  And my interaction with math and logic is purely journeyman.                  

● Plain version, with your repo as the example.

  What happens today

  You ask: "which of my stored beliefs mention files that no longer exist?"
  There are 10,000 paths. Budget is 1,000 steps.

  The evaluator checks maybe 500 paths, runs out, and hands back:

  - must: the stale ones it actually found
  - may: None — it never established a bound, so you can't conclude anything
  about the rest
  - residual: "same question, for the 9,500 paths I didn't reach"

  Now ask again next turn. Fresh budget — and it starts over at path 1. Redoes
  the same 500. You can ask forever and never finish. That's the stall.

  What changes

  The residual is already a formula, not an error string. So store it, and next 
  time ask the residual instead of the original question.

  Pass 1 covers 500, residual holds 9,500. Pass 2 covers the next 500, residual
  holds 9,000. Twenty passes and it's done — and every individual pass still 
  obeyed its 1,000-step budget and never hung.

  That's the whole trick: a question too big for one budget becomes answerable
  across many budgets. Bounded work per turn, unbounded progress across turns.
  Nothing about the guarantee changes; you just stop throwing away what you
  learned.

  Why "simplified" is the interesting word

  If the residual only ever gets shorter by what you processed, that's just
  bookkeeping — 500 at a time, 20 turns, fine.

  But the residual is a formula, and formulas collapse when you learn things.
  Say the residual contains:

  (mentions(f_812) ∧ ¬exists(f_812)) ∨ (mentions(f_813) ∧ ¬exists(f_813)) ∨ …

  Then on some later pass you learn ¬mentions(f_813). That entire branch is now
  provably false and vanishes. You didn't do the work for f_813 — a fact you
  picked up elsewhere killed it for free.

  So the remaining work can shrink faster than the work you're doing. One fact
  can knock out hundreds of branches at once. That's the difference between
  chipping away and actually converging.

  The honest framing on halting

  This does not beat the halting problem, and I want to be exact about what it
  does instead.

  The halting problem says you can't tell in advance whether an arbitrary
  computation finishes. Still true. But there's a different property that's
  nearly as useful and is actually achievable: a measure that strictly 
  decreases.

  If every pass makes the residual strictly smaller, and it can't shrink forever
  without hitting zero, then it will finish. You don't know when. But you know
  it's converging, and you can watch it converge. Compare the two failure modes:

  - Naive timeout: might spin forever with nothing to show, and you can't tell
  the difference between "almost done" and "hopeless."
  - Shrinking residual: guaranteed to finish, unknown when, and the shrink rate
  tells you which situation you're in.

  And here's the part I like: for a genuinely undecidable question — ∀n:Nat. 
  halts(n) — the residual won't shrink. That's not a failure, it's a diagnosis.
  The system can tell you "three passes, zero progress on this one," which is
  real information you can act on. A stalled measure is a signal; a hung process
  is nothing.

  The defect this exposes in what I just built

  Worth saying plainly: my current residual_tail is wrong for this.

  It builds the residual by substituting each remaining path into the body and
  OR-ing them together. So for your 10,000-path query it returns a 9,500-way 
  disjunction — a formula vastly larger than the question you asked. Re-asking
  that is slower than starting over. The measure would go up, not down.

  The fix is to keep the residual quantified — "the same ∃f:Path question, over
  Path minus the 500 I did" — rather than unrolling it into a giant flat
  formula. That needs a domain representation that can carry exclusions, which
  the current Domain::Sort(sym) can't express.

  That's the first thing to build if we go down this road, and it's a
  precondition for everything above.

● Good catch, and the inconsistency is real. Let me be precise about what

  Formula is unrestricted — you can write the exclusion as a conjunct. What's
  closed is Domain, the little enum saying what a quantifier ranges over. So
  it's an implementation wart, not a logic limit. But that's exactly the shape
  of error I keep making, so let me actually check the rest instead of
  reassuring you.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Here's the honest audit. You caught one; there are eight, and one is worse
  than the one you caught.

  The error has a signature

  Every instance is the same thing: a closed Rust enum with no escape hatch, or 
  a const that isn't derived from the budget. Formula itself is genuinely open —
  I was careful there. Everything around it is where I quietly re-introduced
  ceilings. That's a rule you can check me against.

  Ranked

  1. No recursion. This is a genuine expressiveness hole, not a wart.

  Formula has no fixpoint operator. There is no way to write depends*(X, Y) —
  transitive closure, reachability, "everything downstream of this module." The
  artifact lists exactly that as a worked query (Fig. 6, "Everything that
  depends on anything mentioning the compaction planner") and the implementation
  cannot express it. Datalog recursion was in the original design and never
  made it into the term language. Every other item on this list is a bounded
  quantity being wrong; this one is a whole capability missing.

  2. PROBE = 64 — proven broken above. Probe depth for infinite domains is a
  hardcoded constant instead of being spent from the budget. ∀n:Nat. n ≤ 100 is
  refutable in 101 steps and returns Unknown at any budget. The fix is one line
  — probe until the budget says stop — and it makes the infinity results
  strictly stronger.

  3. Domain is closed — the one you caught. Sort | Integers | Naturals. Can't
  say: a sort minus some elements, a union of sorts, a sort carved out by a
  predicate, rationals, an explicit finite set. The general fix subsumes all of
  them: Domain::Where(Box<Domain>, Box<Formula>) — a domain refined by a
  formula. That single variant also unblocks the shrinking-residual work from
  last turn.

  4. Powerset enumeration caps at 63 tuples because I used a u64 bitmask. So
  second-order search over arity 2 with 8 entities (64 tuples) refuses
  regardless of budget. That's a representation choice masquerading as a limit —
  needs a general bitset.

  5. Literal is closed. Int | Fixed | Text. No booleans, no bytes, no big
  integers. And i64 overflow in arithmetic silently degrades to Unknown — sound,
  but it means a computation quietly stops being answerable at the boundary
  rather than saying so.

  6. The second-order "full powerset" isn't quite full. relevant_universe builds
  the universe from sorts named by quantifiers in the body, plus constants
  appearing in it. An entity that belongs in a candidate relation but is
  mentioned nowhere in the query is invisible. So it's the full powerset of a 
  derived universe — which I should have said, and didn't.

  7. Term::Quote is inert. You can store told(sarah, ⟨prefers(adam, tabs)⟩).
  Nothing can ever look inside it. There's no rule connecting a quoted formula
  to its truth — arguably correct (you shouldn't auto-believe what you're told),
  but there's no mechanism to do it deliberately either.

  8. Eq is syntactic. Two skolems that are the same person compare unequal
  forever. The design says identity is an ordinary proposition accruing evidence
  — and the evaluator never reads those is(skolem, entity) facts to rewrite
  terms. So the skolem machinery is representable and non-functional.

  What I'd actually do

  Domain::Where and the PROBE fix are both small and both remove a
  stated-but-false promise — those first. The powerset bitset is mechanical.
  Recursion is the real design work and it's the one that matters, because
  without it a large fraction of what you'd actually ask a codebase memory
  ("what breaks if I change this") is unaskable.

  Want me to take them in that order, or go straight at recursion?

      301 +/// Compute the least fixpoint of `def` by naive iteration.   
      302 +///                                                           
      303 +/// Monotone definitions converge and the result is marked com
          +plete. A negative                                             
      304 +/// occurrence of the recursive predicate makes the operator n
          +on-monotone, so no                                            
      305 +/// least fixpoint is guaranteed — rather than compute a wrong
          + one, the relation                                            
      306 +/// is returned incomplete and everything downstream degrades 
          +to `Unknown`.                                                 
      307 +fn fixpoint(                                                  
      308 +    p: Var,                                                   
      309 +    params: &[(Var, Domain)],                                 
      310 +    def: &Formula,                                            
      311 +    env: &Env,                                                
      312 +    s: &dyn Structure,                                        
      313 +    budget: &mut Budget,                                      
      314 +) -> BoundRel {                                               
      315 +    let monotone = !occurs_negatively(def, p, false);         
      316 +                                                              
      317 +    let mut doms: Vec<(Var, Vec<Term>)> = Vec::new();         
      318 +    let mut exact = true;                                     
      319 +    for (v, d) in params {                                    
      320 +        match enumerate(d, *v, env, s, budget) {              
      321 +            Some(e) => {                                      
      322 +                exact &= e.exact;                             
      323 +                doms.push((*v, e.members));                   
      324 +            }                                                 
      325 +            None => return BoundRel { tuples: Relation::new(),
          + complete: false },                                           
      326 +        }                                                     
      327 +    }                                                         
      328 +    let order: Vec<Var> = doms.iter().map(|(v, _)| *v).collect
          +();                                                           
      329 +    let candidates = product(&doms);                          
      330 +                                                              
      331 +    let mut rel = Relation::new();                            
      332 +    let mut complete = false;                                 
      333 +    loop {                                                    
      334 +        if budget.exhausted() {                               
      335 +            break;                                            
      336 +        }                                                     
      337 +        let mut next = rel.clone();                           
      338 +        for cand in &candidates {                             
      339 +            if budget.exhausted() {                           
      340 +                break;                                        
      341 +            }                                                 
      342 +            let mut scoped = env.clone();                     
      343 +            for (v, t) in cand {                              
      344 +                scoped.vars.insert(*v, t.clone());            
      345 +            }                                                 
      346 +            // Inside the iteration the current approximation 
          +is treated as                                                 
      347 +            // total; that is what makes naive evaluation conv
          +erge to the least                                             
      348 +            // fixpoint for a monotone body.                  
      349 +            scoped                                            
      350 +                .preds                                        
      351 +                .insert(p, BoundRel { tuples: rel.clone(), com
          +plete: monotone });                                           
      352 +            if check(def, &scoped, s, budget).value == Tri::Tr
          +ue {                                                          
      353 +                next.insert(order.iter().map(|v| cand[v].clone
          +()).collect());                                               
      354 +            }                                                 
      355 +        }                                                     
      356 +        if next == rel {                                      
      357 +            complete = true;                                  
      358 +            break;                                            
      359 +        }                                                     
      360 +        rel = next;                                           
      361 +    }                                                         
      362 +                                                              
      363 +    BoundRel { tuples: rel, complete: complete && monotone && 
          +exact }                                                       
      364 +}                                                             
      365 +                                                              
      366 +/// Whether `p` appears under an odd number of negations.     
      367 +fn occurs_negatively(f: &Formula, p: Var, neg: bool) -> bool {
      368 +    match f {                                                 
      369 +        Formula::PredApp(v, _) => *v == p && neg,             
      370 +        Formula::Not(inner) => occurs_negatively(inner, p, !ne
          +g),                                                           
      371 +        Formula::And(fs) | Formula::Or(fs) => {               
      372 +            fs.iter().any(|x| occurs_negatively(x, p, neg))   
      373 +        }                                                     
      374 +        Formula::Forall(_, _, b)                              
      375 +        | Formula::Exists(_, _, b)                            
      376 +        | Formula::ForallPred(_, _, b)                        
      377 +        | Formula::ExistsPred(_, _, b) => occurs_negatively(b,
          + p, neg),                                                     
      378 +        Formula::LetRec(_, _, d, sc) => {                     
      379 +            occurs_negatively(d, p, neg) || occurs_negatively(
          +sc, p, neg)                                                   
      380 +        }                                                     
      381 +        _ => false,                                           
      382 +    }                                                         
      383 +}

● All eight fixed. 43 tests pass, workspace clean.

  #: 1
  Fix: Recursion — Formula::LetRec(pred, params, def, scope), least fixpoint by
  naive iteration
  Cost: BoundRel { tuples, complete } — a cut-short fixpoint is
  under-approximate, so PredApp returns True/Unknown, never False. Non-monotone
   definitions (recursive pred under negation) are detected and  marked
  incomplete rather than computing a wrong fixpoint.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Fix: Probe depth from budget
  Cost: Loop on budget.remaining() > reserve, holding back 64 steps so the
  abstract phase can still run. Integers now alternate outward from zero so
  counterexamples on either side surface early.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Fix: Domain::Where(base, formula)
  Cost: Union, difference, exclusion, predicate-carved subsets are now all just
  formulas. Also added Domain::Set.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Fix: Powerset odometer
  Cost: Vec<bool> instead of a u64 mask. Ceiling is budget, not word size.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Fix: Literal::Bool / Bytes
  Cost: —
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Fix: Structure::entities()
  Cost: Universe is the structure's, not the query's.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Fix: Formula::Holds(term)
  Cost: Descends into a Quote. Kept explicit — hearing a claim still isn't
  believing it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8
  Fix: Structure::canonical()
  Cost: Union-find over is(a,b). Eq and atom lookup both go through
  representatives.

  Two that were subtler than they looked.

  Fix 3 also fixed the residual problem from last turn. narrowed() now returns
  Exists(v, Where(Path, v ∈ rest), body) instead of unrolling — the test asserts
  the residual stays under 4× the original size and is still a Formula::Exists,
  so it's re-askable as-is. That's the precondition for the decreasing-measure
  idea.

  Fix 1 needed enumerate() to report exactness separately, because a
  Where-refined domain whose filter returns Unknown is an over-approximation.
  Anything quantified over an inexact domain now degrades to Unknown rather than
  claiming a decided answer over a superset.

  Storage side too — RelationalView implements entities() and canonical(), with
  is(a, b) as a well-known predicate. Two new store tests: transitive closure
  over stored depends facts (cli → agent → memory → logic), and is(chinmay, 
  chinmay_mehta) flipping an atom from Unknown to True.

  One instance of the pattern survives, weakly. MAX_PROBE = 1_000_000 is still a
  hardcoded ceiling on probe depth — a backstop against spinning for minutes
  when the abstraction would settle it instantly. At budgets above ~2M steps it
  binds before the budget does. It's the same shape of error, much smaller; say
  the word and it comes out.

❯ So now this machinery is complete for expressing the entire range of the      
  universe?                                                                     

● No. And since I've now claimed completeness twice and been wrong twice, let me
  give you the method rather than a third claim.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed against the source. Here's the honest answer, and more usefully, the
  method.

  The method (so you don't have to trust me)

  Both times I claimed completeness and was wrong, the same thing broke it: I 
  never tried to write an actual query. Domain was fine until you needed a
  residual, and then it wasn't. So the test is — pick a real question, try to
  write it, and see what you can't type.

  Let me do that now, out loud.

  What is genuinely complete

  One claim survives, precisely stated: over a finite structure, FO + least 
  fixpoint + full second-order quantification captures every property of that 
  structure definable in the type hierarchy. That's not hedging — it's exact,
  it's Fagin/Stockmeyer, and it means the gaps below aren't "properties we can't
  reach." They're either things not in the model at all, or things expressible
  but absurd to evaluate.

  Trying to write four real queries

  "What did I believe about this module before the refactor?" — can't write it.
  There is no time in the logic at all. No temporal operator, no timestamp term,
  and stmt.created_at and the live flag are invisible to the evaluator. You can
  reify it — add a time argument, use Leq on integers — but nothing supports
  it, and supersession history is currently unreachable. For a memory system
  this is the real hole, and it's the one I'd fix first.

  "How many times did Adam correct me about this file?" — technically writable,
  practically not. Cardinality comparison is ∃SO-expressible (guess an
  injection), so the second-order layer does reach it. But the evaluator would
  walk relation-space to do arithmetic a counter does in one pass. It presents 
  as an expressiveness gap and is really an efficiency one — needs a counting
  primitive, not more logic.

  "Define 'core module' as anything three or more things depend on, then use it 
  twice." — partly writable. LetRec with a non-recursive body is a let-binding
  for predicates, so naming a derived concept works. What's missing is λ at the
  term level: no Term::Lambda, so you cannot construct or pass a function value.
  The artifact's Fig. 1 grammar claims λx:τ. t and the implementation doesn't
  have it. Stated-but-absent, same category as recursion was.

  "Files whose name contains 'compaction'" — the artifact's own Fig. 6 query.
  Literal::Text is opaque: no concatenation, no matching, no substring. Works
  only if the structure interprets in as an uninterpreted predicate. Encodable,
  unsupported.

  The rest

  Real numbers (fixed-point only, deliberate). Uncertainty as a proposition
  (lives in the ledger, outside). Defaults and non-monotonic reasoning —
  "normally X unless Y" — where negation-as-failure via is_closed is all you
  get. Modality and counterfactuals, encodable by materialising possible worlds,
  which nothing does.

  So

  "Complete for the entire range of the universe" — no, and I don't think that
  phrase has a stable meaning for a representation language. What's true: it's
  complete for properties of the world as currently modelled, and the world as
  currently modelled has no time in it.

  The ranked list if you want to keep going: time (genuine hole, and memory is
  fundamentally about change), counting primitive (efficiency wearing an
  expressiveness costume), term-level λ (in the design, not in the code),
  structured literals.

  Want me to take time first? It's the one where the current design is actively
  wrong rather than merely incomplete — supersession is a flag the logic can't
  see, so "what did I used to believe" is unaskable of a system whose whole job
  is remembering.

● Workspace green — 728 passing, exit 0.

  For wV:p4: both errors were real but transient — an intermediate state where
  I'd added Formula::At and Domain::Instants to the enums and hadn't updated the
  evaluator yet. Fixed in the same turn; eval.rs:165 and :702 both cover the
  new variants now. I also broke and fixed a second thing they'd have hit next:
  store.rs:52 hardcoded Some(1) for the schema version, which my bump to 2
  tripped. It now reads schema::SCHEMA_VERSION so a legitimate DDL change
  doesn't require editing the assertion.

  You were right about mnestic — and the original plan was wrong

  reject_txtime_relation checks has_txtime() and nothing else. The plan said
  bitemporality and search were mutually exclusive; that's true of TxTime only.
  A Validity column has no such restriction — verified in mnestic's own tests
  (:create vt_r {k, v: Validity => x: Int}, queried with @ (vt: 'NOW')).

  And valid time is the axis we actually wanted: when the belief held, not when
  the row was written. Conflating the two is what cost the design its memory of
  itself.

  One primitive, not five

  At(Term, Box<Formula>)   // evaluate φ against the structure as it was at t
  Domain::Instants         // the timeline

  That's the whole addition. always, eventually, since are constructors, not 
  evaluator cases — they're ordinary quantification over Instants, so they
  inherit the budget, the two-sided answer, and the residual for free.
  temporal_queries_are_anytime_too pins that: same always query returns
  Unknown+residual at 20 steps and True at unlimited.

  Supersession is now a property, not a hidden flag

  stmt drops live for at: Validity. Retraction is a :put with [t, false] — the
  statement stays in history. So this is now askable, of the real store:

  eventually(prefers(adam, tabs)) ∧ ¬prefers(adam, tabs)

  "Something I used to believe and no longer do." That was unaskable an hour ago
  because liveness was a flag the evaluator couldn't see.
  past_beliefs_survive_retraction walks it end to end: asserted at t=1000,
  retracted at t=2000, At(1500, ...) → True, present → False, superseded → True.

  13 new tests (6 logic temporal, 1 store temporal, plus the 6 earlier fix tests
  re-verified).

  On the philosophical point

  I'll take the correction. "Every limit is either a theorem or a dial" is a
  real criterion — it caught all eight bugs, and it caught this one, since time
  was neither. What I should have said instead of "no stable meaning" is that
  the target is asymptotic and the method is what's stable: pick a question, try
  to type it, see what you can't.

  Remaining from the audit, unchanged: counting primitive (∃SO-expressible,
  absurd to evaluate — efficiency in expressiveness costume), term-level λ,
  structured literals. Next?

  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Item     │                      Implementation                      │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Counting     │ Term::Count(var, domain, body) and Term::Sum(var,        │
  │              │ domain, body, value)                                     │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Term-level λ │ Term::Lambda(params, body) + Formula::Apply(head, args)  │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Structured   │ CONCAT/LEN/SUBSTR functions,                             │
  │ literals     │ CONTAINS/STARTS_WITH/ENDS_WITH predicates, interpreted   │
  │              │ before the structure is consulted                        │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The aggregate is two-sided, which was the non-obvious part. A Count cut short
  isn't Unknown — it's an interval [proven, proven + unresolved], so Leq
  compares intervals and can decide early.
  partial_counts_still_decide_comparisons proves it: "at least 5 corrections"
  over 400 files returns True at 4,000 steps from the lower bound alone, without
  finishing the scan, and honestly Unknown at 6 steps. Same discipline as
  everything else — the aggregate didn't get an exemption.

  Apply unifies intensional and extensional. A Lambda substitutes; a Var bound
  by ExistsPred or LetRec does membership. So an inline-defined predicate and a
  second-order witness are usable in the same position —
  apply_unifies_intensional_and_extensional runs a powerset search that lands on
  Apply(Var(9), ...).

  And the design's own worked query finally runs:
  text_literals_are_no_longer_opaque solves files whose name contains 
  "compaction" and returns both, decided.

  ---
  I found one, and it's worse than inexpressible

  ∃P. ∀n:ℕ. P(n)          truth: True  (take P = ℕ)
                          returns: False

  Definite. Confident. Wrong. In 80µs.

  Not Unknown — a hard False. That breaks the one invariant the whole
  architecture rests on: partial evaluation may only widen toward Unknown, never
  flip a truth value.

  Root cause. ForallPred/ExistsPred take an arity and never a domain. The
  candidate universe comes from relevant_universe, which reads the structure's
  finite entities. So when the body quantifies over Naturals, the second-order
  quantifier silently ranges over subsets of the finite entity set instead of
  subsets of ℕ — then "exhausts the powerset" and reports known(universal), a
  genuine universal about a domain nobody asked about.

  Two failure modes, both bad:

  ┌──────────────────┬───────────┬─────────┬───────┐
  │      Query       │ Structure │ Returns │ Truth │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────┼─────────┼───────┤
  │ ∃P. ∀n:ℕ. P(n)   │ 1 entity  │ False   │ True  │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────┼─────────┼───────┤
  │ ∃P. P(1) ∧ ¬P(2) │ 1 entity  │ False   │ True  │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────┼─────────┼───────┤
  │ same             │ empty     │ Unknown │ True  │
  └──────────────────┴───────────┴─────────┴───────┘

  Why this one specifically

  It's precisely where my completeness argument was load-bearing and I never
  checked the boundary.

  I argued for full standard semantics because over a finite domain every subset
  is first-order definable, so Henkin general models already contain the whole
  powerset and the distinction is vacuous. That argument is correct, and correct
  only for finite structures. Over ℕ the powerset is uncountable, no
  enumeration exists, and Henkin vs standard genuinely diverges — it's the
  difference between second-order arithmetic being complete or not.

  The code inherited "finite" as an unstated assumption from an argument that
  stated it. Domain::Naturals was added for first-order quantification,
  check_infinite_forall handles it properly there, and the second-order path
  never learned that domains could be infinite.

  So the answer to your question: second-order quantification over an infinite 
  domain. And it doesn't fail by refusing to type — it types fine and the
  evaluator lies.

  The fix has a design decision in it

  ForallPred(Var, usize, ...) needs to become ForallPred(Var, Vec<Domain>, ...)
  — arity generalized to a domain per argument position. Then over an infinite
  base the honest verdict is:

  - ∃P over subsets of ℕ: Unknown unless a witness is found among
  named/definable relations (searching an uncountable powerset is not a budget
  problem, it's an impossibility)
  - ∀P over subsets of ℕ: Unknown unless a counterexample is found

  Never known(universal) from exhaustion, because exhaustion cannot happen.
  That's a case where the limit really is a theorem rather than a dial — and the
  current code claims the dial.

  This is the highest-severity thing found all session; everything prior was a
  missing capability, this is an unsound answer. Want me to take it now?

● Soundness bug fixed. 60 tests, 0 failures.

  ForallPred/ExistsPred now carry Vec<Domain> — one domain per argument position
  — and the fix hinges on a single flag: exhaustion only counts when the search
  was exhaustive.

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │            Query             │    Before     │         Now          │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ ∃P. ∀n:ℕ. P(n)               │ False (wrong) │ Unknown + residual   │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ ∃P. P(1) ∧ ¬P(2) over ℕ      │ False (wrong) │ True — witness found │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ ∃P. P(a) ∧ ¬P(a) over finite │ False         │ False (unchanged)    │
  └──────────────────────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  The second row is the part I'm happiest about: a witness is sound over an
  infinite base because a witness is a witness. The body only tests finitely
  many points, so searching the points it names finds one. Only the negative
  direction had to weaken. Three regression tests pin all three rows, including
  that the fix didn't buy soundness by making everything Unknown.

  ---
  The audit: what "provably maximal" can mean

  You can't prove "expresses everything" without fixing what everything means.
  But you can prove maximality against a named yardstick, and there's a good
  one.

  The claim I can actually make: this language expresses all of second-order 
  arithmetic (Z₂). I just verified the characteristic axiom types and evaluates
  — the induction schema, ∀P⊆ℕ. (P(0) ∧ ∀n. P(n)→P(n+1)) → ∀n. P(n), returns
  Unknown rather than lying. Everything Z₂ needs is present: ℕ as a domain,
  0/successor/+/×, equality and ≤, full first-order quantification over ℕ, and
  quantification over sets and relations of naturals.

  That matters because essentially all of classical analysis and number theory 
  formalizes in Z₂ — reals as Cauchy sequences or Dedekind cuts are sets of
  naturals, so continuity, limits, measure, and convergence are all reachable by
  standard coding. My earlier claim that "no reals" was a hole was wrong at the
  level that counts: ℝ isn't a Domain variant, but it doesn't need to be.

  Three separate maximality results now hold, each exact:

  - Finite structures: SO captures the polynomial hierarchy, SO+LFP reaches
  PSPACE. Both present.
  - Arithmetic: the full arithmetical hierarchy (Σ⁰ₙ/Π⁰ₙ, all n) via
  quantification over ℕ.
  - Analysis: the analytical hierarchy (Σ¹ₙ) via quantification over sets of
  naturals.

  The exact ceiling

  Third order. Domain has no Relations variant, so a predicate variable's
  arguments are always individuals. You cannot quantify over sets of sets.
  That's the boundary between Z₂ and full higher-order arithmetic — and it's
  ZFC-strength mathematics on the other side.

  It is a dial, not a theorem: adding Domain::Relations(Vec<Domain>) makes the
  type tower genuinely unbounded, and the evaluator already has the machinery
  (candidates would be built recursively, exhaustion would stay non-exhaustive
  above the finite case, so the soundness discipline carries over unchanged).

  Two smaller items I'd stop calling holes: vagueness (genuinely not truth-apt —
  a modelling decision, not a gap) and Sym = u32 capping entities at ~4.3
  billion, which is a one-character change.

  So the ranking for the maximality push: Domain::Relations is the one thing
  standing between here and "every proposition of higher-order arithmetic." Want
  that next?
